### Q65. JEE Main 2023 (10 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>Given below are two statements: one is labelled as <strong>Assertion A</strong> and the other is labelled as <strong>Reason R</strong>.</p><p><strong>Assertion A</strong> : The energy required to form <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>from <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> is much higher than that required to produce <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math></p><p><strong>Reason R</strong>:<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> is small ion and carry more charge than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math></p><p>In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below.</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both <strong>A</strong> and <b>R</b> are true but <b>R</b> is <strong>NOT</strong> the correct explanation of <b>A</b>
- **(B)** <strong>A</strong> is true but <strong>R</strong> is false
- **(C)** <strong>A</strong> is false but <strong>R</strong> is true
- **(D)** Both <strong>A</strong> and <strong>R</strong> are true and <strong>R</strong> is the correct explanation of <strong>A</strong>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Both <strong>A</strong> and <strong>R</strong> are true and <strong>R</strong> is the correct explanation of <strong>A</strong>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Mg have <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn></math> electrons in valance shell and after donation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math>electrons it converts into <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> ion and attains inert gas configuration, now to ionize one more electron from inert gas configuration is very difficult, hence it is easy to ionize <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> to<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>.  <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>IE</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is always greater than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>IE</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> because on removal of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></math>, size decreases and effective nuclear charge increases.So Assertion is correct.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> is a smaller ion than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math> and has a greater charge density. This means that <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> has a stronger attraction to its electrons than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math> and requires more energy to remove an electron from it. So reason is also correct.</p>

</details>

---

### Q66. JEE Main 2023 (08 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The correct order of electronegativity for given elements is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Br</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>At</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Br</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>At</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Br</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>At</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>At</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Br</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Br</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>At</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>On the periodic table, electronegativity generally increases as you move from left to right across a period and decreases as you move down a group. As a result, the most electronegative elements are found on the top right of the periodic table, while the least electronegative elements are found on the bottom left.</p><p>Element             Electronegativity</p><p>P                         <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>1</mn></math></p><p>C                           <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn></math></p><p>Br                          <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>0</mn></math>                   </p><p>At                           <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>2</mn></math></p><p>Hence, the correct order is Br &gt; C &gt; At &gt; P.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q67. JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

Group-<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>13</mn></math> elements react with <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> in amorphous form to form oxides of type <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mo>=</mo></math> element). Which among the following is the most basic oxide?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Al</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Tl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Ga</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Tl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>The basicity of an oxide is closely related to the electropositive character of the element. The more electropositive the element, the more basic its oxide will be. This is because the oxide ion can react with a cation to form a basic oxide. As we move down Group <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>13</mn></math>, the electropositive character of the elements increases, and so does the basicity of their oxides.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><msub><mi>Al</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><msub><mi>Ga</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><msub><mi>In</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#60;</mo><msub><mi>Tl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></p><p>So most basic oxide among the given options is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Tl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>.</p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q68. JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Which one of the following elements will remain as liquid inside pure boiling water?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Br</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Li</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cs</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>Gallium (Ga) remains as liquid inside boiling water, and the other elements mentioned in options can react with water.</p><p>Bromine is a halogen that reacts readily with water to form hydrobromic acid and hypobromous acid, which can further react to form bromic acid. Lithium and Caesium are alkali metals that react violently with water, producing hydrogen gas and the corresponding metal hydroxide.</p></blockquote>

</details>

xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ca</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Sr</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ba</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ra</mi></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>IE <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi><mo>)</mo></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>899</mn></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>737</mn></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>590</mn></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>549</mn></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>503</mn></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>509</mn></math></td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>In the first transition series, the first and second ionization enthalpy values increase slowly on moving across the period. As the nuclear charge rises, the interaction between the nucleus and valence electrons increases, and as a result, the ionization enthalpy increases. So, Sc has the lowest value of ionization enthalpy while Zn has the highest value of ionization enthalpy.</p><p>So, both assertion and reasons are true and reason is the correct expalantion of assertion.</p><p> </p>

</details>

### Q71. JEE Main 2023 (31 Jan Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The correct increasing order of the ionic radii is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>For isoelectronic species, as nuclear charge increases radius decreases. Greater the positive charge, the lesser the size of the ion. Greater the negative charge, the larger the size of an ion.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#60;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#60;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mspace linebreak="newline"/><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>20</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>19</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>17</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>16</mn><mspace linebreak="newline"/><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>18</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>18</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>18</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>18</mn></math></p><p>So, the correct option is (D).</p>

</details>

### Q72. JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Bond dissociation energy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></math> bond of the &quot;<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi></math>&quot; hydrides of group <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>16</mn></math> elements (given below), follows order.
(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math>
(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi></math>
(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Se</mi></math>
(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Te</mi></math>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Bond strength is inversely proportional to the bond length of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></math> bond of hydrides of 16th group hydrides. The bond length increases down the group. Hence, bond dissociation enthalpy decreases down the group.</p><p>Hence, bond dissociation energy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></math> bond in hydrides of group 16 follows the order</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi>Se</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi>Te</mi></math>.</p>

</details>

---

### Q73. JEE Main 2023 (29 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>Given below are two statements:</p><p><strong>Statement I:</strong> The decrease in first ionization enthalpy from <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> is much larger than that from <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math>.</p><p><strong>Statement II:</strong> The d orbitals in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math> are completely filled.</p><p>In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Statement I is incorrect but statement II is correct.
- **(B)** Both the statements I and II are correct
- **(C)** Statement I is correct but statement II is incorrect
- **(D)** Both the statements I and II are incorrect

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) Both the statements I and II are correct**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>First ionization enthalpy (IE<sub>1</sub>) of B, Al, Ga are 801, 577 and 579 kJ/mol respectively. </p><p>As decrease in first ionization enthalpy is much larger from B to Al. <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8710;</mo><msub><mi>IE</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> of B and Al is greater than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8710;</mo><msub><mi>IE</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> of Al and Ga. So, the statement I is correct.</p><p>This is due to completely filled d-orbitals in Ga and d-electron have low screening effect to compensate the increase in nuclear charge. So, The statement II is also correct.</p><p>Hence, the correct option is B.</p><p> </p>

</details>

---

### Q74. JEE Main 2022 (29 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The first ionization enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Mg</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Si</mi></math>, respectively, are: <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>496</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>737</mn></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>786</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><msup><mrow><mi>mo</mi><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>. The first ionization enthalpy <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><msup><mi>kJmol</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></mfenced></math> of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>487</mn></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>768</mn></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>577</mn></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>856</mn></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>577</mn></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"></math>In the third period, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> comes after <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> and before <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Si</mi></math>. Since in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>6</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></math> ), the last electron is to be lost from a fully filled <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></math>-orbital while in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>ls</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>6</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>1</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo></math>) it is to be lost from a <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></math>-orbital, therefore, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mo>&#8710;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> is lower than that of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>737</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>mol</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>). Further, nuclear charge increases in going from <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math>, therefore <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mo>&#8710;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> is higher than that of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi></math>(<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>496</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>mol</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>) but lower than that of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi></math> (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>737</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>mol</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>). Therefore, the appropriate value of ionisation potential of  <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>577</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>mol</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup></math>.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q75. JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The correct decreasing order for metallic character is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Na</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>A metal is a material that, when freshly prepared, polished, or fractured, shows a lustrous appearance, and conducts electricity and heat relatively well. Metals are typically malleable (they can be hammered into thin sheets) or ductile (can be drawn into wires)</p><p>On moving top to bottom of the periodic table metallic character increases and on moving left to right metallic character decreases.</p><p>Metallic character is directly proportional to the atomic size.</p><p>The correct order of atomic size of the given elements is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>.</p><p>Hence, correct order of metallic character is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Si</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math></p><p>All these elements are form 3rd period except beryllium which is from second period</p><p>Na is an alkali metal</p><p>Mg and Be are alkaline earth metals, </p><p>Phosphorous belongs to a group which is commonly called as pnicogens.</p><p>Si belongs to 14th group</p><p> </p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q76. JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>In which of the following pairs, electron gain enthalpies of constituent elements are nearly the same or identical?</p><p>(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Rb</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cs</mi></math></p><p>(B) Na and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi></math></p><p>(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ar</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Kr</mi></math></p><p>(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>At</mi></math></p><p>Choose the correct answer from the options given below</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></math> only
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math> only
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math> only
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math> only

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math> only**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Rb</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Cs</mi></math> have nearly same electron gain enthalpy Their values are nearly <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>46</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ar</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#38;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Kr</mi></math> also have same <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>&#916;H</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">g</mi><mo>.</mo></mrow></msub></math>. Value is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>96</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi></math>.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>and</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi></math> have different values <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>53</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>and</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>48</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo></math>. And <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>and</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>At</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>has</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>values</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>295</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>and</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>270</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi></math>. </p><p>As we move down the group value of electron gain enthalpy decreases, with few exceptions.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q77. JEE Main 2022 (27 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Outermost electronic configurations of four elements <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math> are given below:</p><p>(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></math></p><p>(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math></p><p>(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math></p><p>(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>4</mn></msup></math></p><p>The correct order of first ionization enthalpy for them is</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>Mg</mi></math></p><p>(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>1</mn></msup><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>Al</mi></math></p><p>(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math></p><p>(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>4</mn></msup><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi></math> has one unpaired electron in its highest energy orbital <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></mrow></mfenced></math>, and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#39;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></math> highest energy orbital <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mrow><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> has the paired electrons.</p><p>It is energetically favourable for all the electrons in an orbital to be paired, which means that breaking up this pair would require more energy.</p><p>Compared to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>16</mn><mi>th</mi></math> group, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>15</mn></math> group have high ionisation energy. It is because of the stable half filled configuration of he <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>15</mn><mi>th</mi></math> group elements.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>4</mn></msup></math></p><p><img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/2ce6072d-74cb-417a-be7b-08558eb0330d-image.png" style="width: 246px; height: 50px;" /></p><p>Therefore, P has more ionisation energy than S.</p><p>Hence, the correct order of first ionization enthalpy is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q78. JEE Main 2022 (27 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The incorrect statement is

**Options:**

- **(A)** The first ionization enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi></math> is less than that of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Na</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Li</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Xe</mi></math> does not have the lowest first ionization enthalpy in its group
- **(C)** The first ionization enthalpy of element with atomic number <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>37</mn></math> is lower than that of the element with atomic number <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>38</mn></math>.
- **(D)** The first ionization enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math> is higher than that of the d-block element with atomic number <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>30</mn></math>.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) The first ionization enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math> is higher than that of the d-block element with atomic number <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>30</mn></math>.**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p> Ionisation enthalpy of alkali metals decreases down the group from Li to Cs. This is because of increase in size which outweighs the increasing nuclear charge, and the outermost electron is very well screened from the nuclear charge.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Li</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Na</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi></math></p><p>Due to stable electronic configuration noble gases exhibit very high ionisation enthalpy. However, it decreases down the group with increase in atomic size.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>He</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Ne</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Kr</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Xe</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Rn</mi></math></p><p>Alkaline earths have higher ionization enthalpy values than alkali metals. This is because the atomic size of alkaline earths is smaller than that of alkali metals.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Sr</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Rb</mi></math></p><p>Because of completely filled configuration, zinc is stable and requires higher ionization energy whereas gallium is yet to fill its p-orbitals, so it has lower ionization energy compared to zinc.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Zn</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Ga</mi></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q79. JEE Main 2022 (26 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Given two statements below :</p><p>Statement I : In <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> molecule the covalent radius is double of the atomic radius of chlorine.</p><p>Statement II : Radius of anionic species is always greater than their parent atomic radius.</p><p>Choose the most appropriate answer from options given below </p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both Statement I and Statement II are correct.
- **(B)** Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect.
- **(C)** Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect.
- **(D)** Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct.**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>In <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> molecule, the covalent radius is half of the internuclear distance, so statement <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced></math> is false. The covalent radius is little smaller than the actual radius of the atom of chlorine due to overlapping during formation of covalent bond.</p><p>For the same element, anion has lower effective nuclear charge than atom so, anion is larger than the parent atom. That means statement <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced></math> is correct.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q80. JEE Main 2022 (25 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The first ionization enthalpies of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math> follow the order

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Be</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>The ionisation enthalpy increases as we go from left to right in a period, while it decreases as we come down a group. So according to this, the order will be <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math>. But there are two exceptions, in the case of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></math> because of the highly stable <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></math> (completely filled orbital) and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math> (half-filled orbital <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math>) valence configuration. Due to this, the correct order is:
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Be</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></math>.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q81. JEE Main 2022 (29 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Given below are two statements. One is labelled as
Assertion <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> and the other is labelled as Reason <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math>.
Assertion <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> : The first ionization enthalpy for oxygen is lower than that of nitrogen.</p><p>Reason <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> : The four electrons in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></math> orbitals of oxygen experience more electron-electron repulsion.</p><p>In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below.</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> are correct and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> is the correct explanation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math>.
- **(B)** Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> are correct but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> is NOT the correct explanation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math>.
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> is correct but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> is not correct.
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> is not correct but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> is correct

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> are correct and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></math> is the correct explanation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></math>.**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Ionization energy of oxygen is less than that of nitrogen because <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></math> subshell of nitrogen is half filled, and half filled orbitals have some extra stability, so removing electrons from them will be little more difficult.
And In oxygen atom, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn></math> of the <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>four</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></math> electrons must occupy the same <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi></math> orbital resulting in an increased electron electron-repulsion.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>4</mn></msup></math></p>

</details>

---

### Q82. JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>Match List-I with List-II</p><table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 500px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"> </td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>List-I</strong></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"> </td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>List-II</strong></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(A)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>7</mn></msub></math></td>			<td>(I)</td>			<td>Amphoteric</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(B)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Na</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></td>			<td>(II)</td>			<td>Basic</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(C)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Al</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></td>			<td>(III)</td>			<td>Neutral</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(D)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></td>			<td>(IV)</td>			<td>Acidic</td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>Choose the correct answer from the options given below</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>IV</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>III</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>IV</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>III</mi></mfenced></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>IV</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>III</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>III</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>IV</mi></mfenced></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>IV</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced><mo>,</mo><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></mfenced><mo>-</mo><mfenced><mi>III</mi></mfenced></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Non-metal oxides are generally acidic in nature.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>7</mn></msub><mo>-</mo></math> Acidic</p><p>Metal oxides are generally basic in nature</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Na</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>-</mo></math> Basic</p><p>Amphoteric oxides reacts with both acids and bases. </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Al</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>-</mo></math> Amphoteric.</p><p>Some non-metal oxides don&#39;t react with both acids and bases they are neutral in nature.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>-</mo></math> Netural</p>

</details>

---

### Q83. JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The correct order of ionic size of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math> is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#60;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>These are all isoelectronic species. More the negative charge on the species, more is the size of that ion and more the positive charge on the species, less is the size of that ion.</p><p>The correct order of ionic radii is<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">N</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Na</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mfenced><mi>Radii</mi></mfenced></math></p><p>(Isoelectronic species)</p><p> </p>

</details>

### Q84. JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Given below are two statements : one is labelled as Assertion and the other is labelled as Reason.</p><p><strong>Assertion:</strong> The ionic radii of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> are same.</p><p><strong>Reason:</strong> Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> are isoelectronic species.</p><p>In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below.</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both Assertion and Reason are true and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion.
- **(B)** Both Assertion and Reason are true but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion.
- **(C)** Assertion is true but Reason is false.
- **(D)** Assertion is false but Reason is true.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Assertion is false but Reason is true.**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></math> have <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>10</mn></math> electrons each and they are isoelectronic species.</p><p>But the size of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup></math> is greater than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> as <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>size</mi><mo>&#8733;</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mstyle displaystyle="true"><mfrac><mi mathvariant="normal">z</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></mfrac></mstyle></mfrac></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mi mathvariant="normal">z</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>12</mn><mn>10</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>2</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mi mathvariant="normal">z</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>8</mn><mn>10</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>8</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfrac><mi mathvariant="normal">z</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mi>Number</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>protons</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>Number</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>electrons</mi></mrow></mfrac></math></p>

</details>

### Q85. JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Which of the following is a metalloid?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Sc</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Pb</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Bi</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Te</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Te</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>The six commonly recognised metalloids are boron, silicon, germanium, arsenic, antimony, and tellurium.</p><p>Hence. tellurium is a metalloid.</p><p>All other options are metals.</p>

</details>

---

### Q86. JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `3`

<p>What is the correct oder of electron gain enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cl</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Te</mi><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Po</mi></math></p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Cl</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Te</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Po</mi><mo>&#160;</mo></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cl</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Te</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Po</mi><mo>&#160;</mo></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Te</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Po</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Cl</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Po</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Te</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Cl</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cl</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Te</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>Po</mi><mo>&#160;</mo></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Electron gain enthalpy values of the elements are given below in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mol</mi></math>,</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtable columnalign="center center center center" columnspacing="1em" rowspacing="4pt"><mtr><mtd><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi></mtd><mtd><mi>Cl</mi></mtd><mtd><mtext>&#160;Te&#160;</mtext></mtd><mtd><mtext>&#160;Po&#160;</mtext></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>333</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>349</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>190</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>174</mn><mo>.</mo></mtd></mtr></mtable></math></p><p>Down the group electron affinity decreases. The size of fluorine atom is small. The addition of electron to small fluorine atom results in electron-electron repulsions. Hence, fluorine has less electron affinity than chlorine.</p><p>Hence, the correct order is </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cl</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">F</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi>Te</mi><mrow></mrow></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><mi>PO</mi></math></p><p> </p>

</details>

### Q87. JEE Main 2022 (24 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Metals generally melt at very high temperatures, Among the following which one has the highest melting point?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Hg</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cs</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Transition metals have high melting point due to their high enthalpy of atomisation. They form strong metallic bonds with unpaired electrons.</p><p>Melting point of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>961</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>&#176;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math></p><p>Melting point of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cs</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>28</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>&#176;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math></p><p>Melting point of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>30</mn><mo>&#176;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math></p><p>Melting point of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Hg</mi><mo>=</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>38</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>83</mn><mo>&#176;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cs</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Ga</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Hg</mi></math> are liquid at or near room temperature.</p><p>Among the given options <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math> has the maximum Melting point, as it belongs to transition metal series.</p>

</details>

### Q88. JEE Main 2021 (31 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

Given below are two statements : one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other is labelled as Reason (R).
Assertion (A) : Metallic character decreases and non-metallic character increases on moving from left to right in a period.
Reason (R): It is due to increase in ionisation enthalpy and decrease in electron gain enthalpy, when one moves from left to right in a period.
In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below :

**Options:**

- **(A)** (A) is false but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> is true.
- **(B)** Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> are correct and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> is the correct explanation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math>
- **(C)** Both <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> are correct but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> is not the correct explanation of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math>.
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math> is true but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> is false.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi></mfenced></math> is true but <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">R</mi></mfenced></math> is false.**

**Detailed Solution:**

It is due to increase in ionisation enthalpy and increases in electron gain enthalpy, when one moves from left to right in a period.

</details>

### Q89. JEE Main 2021 (27 Aug Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The correct order of ionic radii for the ions, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></math> is :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtable columnalign="left"><mtr><mtd/><mtd><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></mtd><mtd><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup></mtd><mtd><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi><mo>-</mo></mtd><mtd><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></mtd><mtd><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mi mathvariant="normal">Z</mi></mtd><mtd><mn>15</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>16</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>17</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>19</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>20</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mi>No</mi><mo>.</mo><mi>of</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></mtd><mtd><mn>18</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>18</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>18</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>18</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>18</mn></mtd></mtr></mtable></math></p><p>For isoelectronic species greater is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Z</mi></math> smaller is radius. So correct order</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Cl</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">K</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#62;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></p>

</details>

### Q90. JEE Main 2021 (27 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The CORRECT order of first ionisation enthalpy is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>
- **(B)** <span style="color:null;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math></span>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>The ionization energy of the elements increases from left to right in the periodic table. The first ionization of magnesium is greater than that of aluminium because magnesium has a stable complete electronic configuration <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>6</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup></math>, so it requires more energy to remove the first electron from it, whereas aluminium has electronic configuration <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>1</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>2</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>6</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">p</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math> which need lesser energy than that of magnesium. Similarly, phosphorus has more ionisation energy than sulphur due to the half filled electronic configuration in the phosphorus.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mi>IE</mi><mo>&#160;.</mo><mi>order</mi><mo>&#160;&#8658;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Mg</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">S</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">P</mi></math></p><p> </p>

</details>

- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>)</mo></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>NaOH</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo></math> Basic
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Be</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>OH</mi><msub><mo>)</mo><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>&#8594;</mo></math> Amphoteric
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ca</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>OH</mi><msub><mo>)</mo><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>&#8594;</mo></math> Basic
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>OH</mi><msub><mo>)</mo><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#8594;</mo></math> Acidic
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>OH</mi><msub><mo>)</mo><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#8594;</mo></math> Amphoteric

</details>
